1.0 INTRODUCTION
The Public Procurement Act No. 8 of 2020 (PPA), was enacted to revise the law relating to procurement so as to enhance transparency, efficiency, effectiveness, economy, value for money,
competition and accountability in public procurement; regulate and control practices relating to public procurement. The PPA was enacted primarily to promote the integrity, fairness and
public confidence in, the procurement process; promote the participation of citizens in public procurement; continue the existence of the Zambia Public Procurement Authority (the Authority)
or incidental to, the foregoing.

In accordance with Section 12 (3) of the PPA, the Authority is required to issue a quarterly Market Price Index (MPI) to be used by a controlling officer, chief executive officer and procuring
entity for purposes of an informed price decision. Further, Section 12 (2) of the PPA provides that a procuring entity shall procure standard goods, services and works in accordance with the
price index issued by the Authority. Additionally, procuring entities will use the MPI as a tool for budgeting, annual procurement planning, and bid/tender evaluation.

The Market Price Index is a metric that represents price position relative to competitors across key categories and brands, suppliers of goods, works and services. The MPI is a key component
in the tender pricing strategy which looks at prices of commonly procured items, specification units of purchase, neutral specification of items or brand names that are standardized items and
procured by procuring entities in different categories described herein.




2. 0 METHODOLOGY
The Zambia Statistics Agency (ZamStats) is responsible for the coordination of production and publication of official statistics in Zambia, in accordance with the Statistics Act No. 13 of 2018.
The market price information in this index was collected and compiled by ZamStats. Further, analysis and validation were conducted by both ZamStats and ZPPA. The Authority has
collaborated with, and will continue to collaborate with ZamStats in the production of the MPI.

        2.1 Coverage
         The MPI data collection exercise is conducted in all the Ten (10) provincial capitals of Zambia. Other districts included in the MPI data collection are Livingstone and Kitwe.

        2.2 Data Collection
        Price data collection is collected in selected retail outlets within towns mentioned above on a quarterly basis. Data collection exercise is carried out by ZamStats staff stationed in
        provincial towns and electronically transmitted to ZamStats HQ and ZPPA for processing. At least three (03) quotations are collected for each item in each province with the maximum
        being six (06) quotations. Some items with one supplier across the country are collected centrally (e.g Medical supplies & Motor Vehicles prices are only collected in Lusaka). The unit
        prices collected are from retail outlets, VAT inclusive, Kwacha denomination and contain the profit margins based on the outlets pricing models unless otherwise stated . The MPI does
        not contain wholesale prices and products or services do not include transport costs unless stated otherwise.

        2.3 Data Analysis
        The data processing phases included data entry, cleaning and analysis. The following variables were computed and employed during the data processing phases: namely, geometric
        mean, standard deviation, coefficient of variation and the count (i.e. number of quotations per item). The maximum and minimum prices for each item were also computed by province
        and at national level.
        ZamStats used some of these variables in the MPI report as explained below:
        The Geometric mean of prices was calculated by getting the product of their values and taking the nth root of the product (as opposed to the arithmetic mean which uses the sum of
        their values divided by n) where n refers to the number of observations. The geometric mean represents the average price and was calculated for each item in each province from a
        minimum number of six price quotations. The ‘National Average’ price was also derived by calculating the geometric mean from the provincial average prices on each item.
        The standard deviation is a measure of dispersion and was calculated to show how far data sets deviate from the group geometric mean.
        The Coefficient of Variation (CV), is a measure of spread that describes the amount of variability relative to the mean and is calculated by dividing the standard deviation by the
        geometric mean and multiplying by 100.
        The Count ‘N’ is the number of valid prices which is used to calculate the geometric mean.




                 2.4 Product Clasification
        The United Nations Standard Products and Services Code (UNSPSC) international coding system was used to code all the items. The UNSPSC coding system enables buyers and sellers
        to describe goods and services in a common way without referring to any suppliers' in-house catalogue codes and descriptions. The UNSPSC framework adopts a four or five level
        classification, using the labels Segment, Family, Class, Commodity and Business Function.

        This index for the 1st quarter of 2026, has 26 categories, 2,559 products and 926 No. of outlets. A total number of 80 data collectors from all the provincial capitals of Zambia including
        Livingstone and Kitwe were involved in data collection.

3.0 APPLICATION OF THE MARKET PRICE INDEX
Procuring entities are advised to refer to the Minimum, Average and Maximum prices of their respective provinces. However, where the price(s) for a particular item(s) are not provided for in
the respective province, the National Average Prices or Price Reasonableness Analysis (PRA) may be used. If the product is not appearing on the MPI published list, PEs are advised to use Price
Reasonableness analysis (PRA) to determine the price. Procuring entities are hereby guided that the tolerable range of prices for goods, works or services in the Market Price Index which is
plus or minus fifteen percent (±15%) of the average prices has now been included in the Minimum and Maximum prices as indicated in the survey results tables.
The MPI shall be used during the following stages:
(a)    Budgeting;
(b)    Annual Procurement Planning
(c)    Bid/Tender Evaluation

The brand names included in the market price index were mentioned as emphasis for most commonly procured goods and services in the public sector. Therefore, the stated brand names are
not an exclusive recommendation or prescription by the Authority to procuring entities, but are merely mentioned for purposes of additional information in the market place as some
products/items are difficult to describe without mentioning brand names.

Procuring entities are reminded to undertake price reasonableness analysis (PRA), subject to approval by the internal approval’s authority, were an item to be procured is not covered in the
quarterly MPI.

All procuring entities are reminded of the provisions of section 12 (5) of the PPA which provides that ‘…An office holder who willfully or negligently engages or is involved in a
transaction in which standard goods, services and works are procured at an inflated price commits an offence…’.

The Authority shall continuously review and update the MPI to ensure that goods, works or services that are commonly procured by procuring entities are covered in the subsequent MPI’s.

PEs are further advised to use current Government published prices and should not refer to out-of-date publications and reports out-of-date.


        3.1 Accessing MPI
        The MPI is accessible on the Authority's website (https://www.zppa.org.zm/home) and on the electronic Government Procurement (e-GP) platform
        (https://eprocure.zppa.org.zmlepps/home.do). The 2026 first (1st) quarter MPI shall be in effect until the Authority issues the next quarterly MPI.




NB:

The blank spaces or dashes found in the product price survey results in the tables below indicates that prices are not shown due to the products /services not being available at the time of data

5.0 CONCLUSION
Price variations are a result of numerous factors listed, but not limited to the ones outlined below:

1. In places distant from Lusaka and the Copperbelt, which are the sources of most product, goods, materials and services, prices may appear
to be higher. However, in places which are near to some border areas, goods, works and service providers may have cheaper sources from
neighboring countries that are able to supply at lower prices;



2. Differences in product brands and services available in certain provinces;

3. Demand and supply of certain products, goods and services;

4. Economical activities in certain geographical areas may influence price variations as compared with those areas with less economic
activities;

5. Industrialization of certain provinces and towns may influence price setting;



7. Some outlying provinces do not have mega chain stores that usually stock most known high-quality brands of certain products; and


8. Some provinces may have very low prices for some products e.g. timber products, due to their proximity to the source of raw materials.




NB: Where an item to be procured is not included in the MPI, or the prices offered by the bidders are justifiably below or higher than the
tolerable (±15%) PE's are advised to undertake Price Reasonableness Analysis (PRA) in accordance with section 12 (4) of the Public
Procurement Act No. 8 of 2020 and Regulation 11 of Public Procurement Regulations of 2022.
